本测试块涵盖臀区、髋关节、腰骶丛神经与异常步态：股神经（L2-L4，支配髂腰肌与股四头肌，膝反射减弱，股前内侧感觉缺失）；闭孔神经（L2-L4，闭孔管卡压，大腿内收肌群瘫痪，内侧皮肤麻木）；臀上神经（支配臀中肌、臀小肌与阔筋膜张肌，Trendelenburg征阳性与摇摆步态）；臀下神经（支配臀大肌，站立起立困难与上楼梯费力）；坐骨神经（梨状肌上孔与下孔穿行，梨状肌综合征与坐骨神经痛）；髋关节骨折（股骨颈囊内骨折损伤旋股内侧动脉导致股骨头缺血性坏死 vs 股骨转子间囊外骨折）。

<a id='question-001'></a>

### Question 001: Femoral Nerve Injury during Hip Screw Placement
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The femoral nerve (L2-L4) roots pass through the psoas major muscle, making it vulnerable to injury during surgical procedures in the hip region, particularly those involving the greater trochanter or placement of hip screws. (股神经 (L2-L4) 根穿行于髂腰肌中，使其在髋部手术中，特别是涉及大转子或髋螺钉置入的手术中易受损伤。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 78-year-old female with a history of osteoporosis presents to the emergency department after sustaining a displaced femoral neck fracture following a fall. Radiographs confirm a comminuted intracapsular fracture. She undergoes an open reduction and internal fixation (ORIF) procedure involving placement of a cephalomedullary nail (hip screw) via a standard anterolateral approach. Postoperatively, she complains of severe anterior thigh pain and inability to extend her right knee or dorsiflex her right ankle. Physical examination reveals complete paralysis of the quadriceps femoris muscle (patellar reflex absent) and weakness in hip flexion (iliopsoas). Sensory examination shows decreased sensation over the anterior and medial aspects of the thigh and medial leg. An MRI performed 2 weeks postoperatively shows edema and potential compression of the femoral nerve within the psoas major muscle at the level of the iliac crest. Which anatomical structure is most likely responsible for the postoperative femoral nerve injury in this patient?

(A) Gluteus medius muscle
(B) Psoas major muscle
(C) Tensor fasciae latae muscle
(D) Iliacus muscle
(E) Sartorius muscle

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 78-year-old female, osteoporosis, femoral neck fracture. This sets the stage for hip surgery.
- **Procedure:** ORIF with cephalomedullary nail (hip screw) via anterolateral approach. This approach involves dissection near the hip joint and potentially manipulation of structures around the greater trochanter.
- **Postoperative Symptoms:** Anterior thigh pain, inability to extend knee (quadriceps paralysis), inability to dorsiflex ankle (tibialis anterior weakness, innervated by femoral nerve), weakness in hip flexion (iliopsoas weakness), sensory loss (anterior/medial thigh, medial leg). These symptoms strongly point to a femoral nerve injury.
- **Physical Exam Findings:** Confirms quadriceps paralysis (patellar reflex absent), iliopsoas weakness, and sensory deficits consistent with the femoral nerve distribution.
- **Imaging:** MRI shows edema and compression of the femoral nerve within the psoas major muscle near the iliac crest. This localizes the site of injury.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the anatomical structure responsible for the injury.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Femoral Nerve Anatomy:** The femoral nerve (L2, L3, L4) is the largest branch of the lumbar plexus. It originates from the posterior divisions of the L2, L3, and L4 spinal nerves.
- **Course through Psoas Major:** After forming within the psoas major muscle, the femoral nerve travels inferiorly and laterally *within* the substance of the psoas major muscle, deep to the iliacus muscle. It exits the psoas major muscle laterally, typically near the iliac crest or slightly below it, passing under the inguinal ligament to enter the thigh.
- **Vulnerability during Hip Surgery:** The anterolateral approach to the hip, often used for hip screw placement, involves dissection through the fascia lata, potentially exposing the iliacus and psoas major muscles. The femoral nerve's course *within* the psoas major makes it susceptible to direct injury, traction, or compression during retraction or screw placement, especially if the screw is placed too proximally or if excessive traction is applied.
- **Clinical Correlation:** Injury to the femoral nerve results in weakness or paralysis of the muscles it innervates: quadriceps femoris (knee extension), sartorius (hip flexion, abduction, external rotation; knee flexion), pectineus (hip adduction), and iliacus (hip flexion, though also innervated by the femoral branch of the obturator nerve). Sensory loss occurs over the anterior and medial thigh and medial leg (via the anterior cutaneous branch). The patellar reflex (L2-L4) is lost. The clinical presentation in the vignette perfectly matches a femoral nerve injury.
- **Kaplan/First Aid Reference:** Kaplan Preclinical Anatomy Review 2023, Chapter 6 (Lower Limb), details the course of the femoral nerve through the psoas major. First Aid 2025, Musculoskeletal section, describes femoral nerve injury as a complication of hip surgery.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Gluteus medius muscle:** This muscle is located on the lateral aspect of the hip, primarily involved in hip abduction and stabilization. It is innervated by the superior gluteal nerve (L4, L5, S1). Injury to this muscle would cause a Trendelenburg gait, not the specific motor and sensory deficits described. It is not directly related to the femoral nerve's course or the surgical approach described.
- (C) **Tensor fasciae latae muscle:** This muscle is located on the lateral hip, deep to the fascia lata. It assists in hip abduction and stabilization and is innervated by the superior gluteal nerve (L4, L5, S1). Like the gluteus medius, injury would not cause the described femoral nerve deficits. It is not in the path of the femoral nerve within the psoas major.
- (D) **Iliacus muscle:** The iliacus muscle originates from the iliac fossa and forms the bulk of the iliopsoas muscle along with the psoas major. The femoral nerve passes *deep* to the iliacus muscle, within the psoas major. While the iliopsoas region is involved, the nerve is specifically located within the psoas major muscle belly at the level of injury described. Injury to the iliacus itself would primarily affect hip flexion (innervated by the femoral and obturator nerves) but wouldn't directly cause the femoral nerve compression described on MRI.
- (E) **Sartorius muscle:** This muscle is located in the anterior thigh and is innervated by the femoral nerve. Injury to the sartorius muscle itself would contribute to the overall femoral nerve deficit but is not the anatomical structure *causing* the nerve injury in this context. The injury is to the nerve *within* the psoas major, proximal to its branching to the sartorius.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure          | Location                                  | Innervation (if applicable) | Relation to Femoral Nerve                                  | Clinical Relevance to Vignette                                                                 |
|--------------------|-------------------------------------------|-----------------------------|------------------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| **Psoas Major**    | Posterior abdominal wall, hip region      | Ventral rami L1-L3         | **Femoral nerve passes *within* its substance.**             | **Most likely site of injury during hip surgery due to nerve's course.**                       |
| Iliacus            | Iliac fossa, hip region                   | Femoral & Obturator nerves | Femoral nerve passes *deep* to it.                         | Less likely site of direct compression compared to psoas major.                             |
| Gluteus Medius     | Lateral hip                               | Superior Gluteal Nerve      | Not related to femoral nerve course.                        | Injury causes Trendelenburg gait, not described symptoms.                                   |
| Tensor Fasciae Latae| Lateral hip                               | Superior Gluteal Nerve      | Not related to femoral nerve course.                        | Injury causes Trendelenburg gait, not described symptoms.                                   |
| Sartorius          | Anterior thigh                             | Femoral Nerve               | Innervated by the femoral nerve, distal to the injury site. | Injury to the muscle itself is a *result* of the nerve injury, not the cause of the injury. |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The femoral nerve (L2-L4) passes through the psoas major muscle, making it vulnerable during hip surgeries, especially those involving the anterolateral approach or proximal screw placement. Remember the specific course of the nerve within the psoas major muscle belly. Key differentials for hip surgery complications include injuries to the superior gluteal nerve (gluteus medius/minimus, TFL), sciatic nerve (posterior thigh muscles, common peroneal/tibial nerves), obturator nerve (adductors), and lateral femoral cutaneous nerve (sensory loss over lateral thigh).

<a id='question-002'></a>

### Question 002: Femoral Nerve Injury
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The femoral nerve (L2-L4) passes deep to the inguinal ligament, through the femoral triangle, and is vulnerable to injury from pelvic fractures, retroperitoneal hematomas, or psoas abscesses due to its anatomical course near the iliopsoas muscle and hip joint capsule. (股神经 (L2-L4) 穿行于腹股沟韧带深面，通过股三角，易受盆骨折、腹膜后血肿或腰大肌脓肿损伤，因为其解剖位置靠近髂腰肌和髋关节囊。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old male presents to the emergency department after sustaining a high-energy motor vehicle accident. He complains of severe right hip pain and inability to extend his right knee against gravity. On physical examination, his right lower extremity demonstrates significant quadriceps weakness (MRC grade 2/5), sensory loss over the anterior thigh and medial leg, and a diminished right patellar reflex. Anteroposterior (AP) and lateral radiographs of the pelvis reveal a comminuted fracture of the right acetabulum with displacement. A subsequent CT scan of the abdomen and pelvis shows a large retroperitoneal hematoma extending from the fracture site, compressing the right psoas muscle. Which of the following structures is most likely injured in this patient?

(A) Obturator nerve
(B) Sciatic nerve
(C) Femoral artery
(D) Femoral nerve
(E) Superior gluteal nerve

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 68-year-old male, high-energy trauma (MVA).
- **Chief Complaint:** Right hip pain, inability to extend right knee.
- **Physical Exam:**
    - Right lower extremity: Quadriceps weakness (knee extension), sensory loss (anterior thigh, medial leg), diminished patellar reflex.
- **Imaging:** Comminuted right acetabular fracture, large retroperitoneal hematoma compressing the right psoas muscle.
- **Deduction:** The combination of acetabular fracture, retroperitoneal hematoma compressing the psoas muscle, quadriceps weakness (knee extension), sensory loss in the anterior thigh/medial leg distribution, and diminished patellar reflex strongly points to injury of the nerve supplying the quadriceps muscle and providing sensation to that area, which is the femoral nerve. The hematoma compressing the psoas muscle is a key clue, as the femoral nerve passes near the psoas muscle.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Femoral Nerve (L2-L4):** Arises from the lumbar plexus (posterior division of L2, L3, L4 ventral rami).
- **Course:** Descends through the psoas major muscle, passes inferior to the inguinal ligament (entering the femoral triangle), lateral to the femoral artery, and deep to the sartorius muscle.
- **Branches:** Supplies motor innervation to the quadriceps femoris (rectus femoris, vastus lateralis, vastus medialis, vastus intermedius - knee extension), sartorius (hip flexion, abduction, external rotation; knee flexion), and pectineus (hip flexion, adduction). Provides sensory innervation to the anterior thigh (via anterior femoral cutaneous branches) and medial leg (via saphenous nerve branch).
- **Reflex:** Mediates the patellar reflex (knee jerk).
- **Clinical Correlation:** Acetabular fractures and associated retroperitoneal hematomas can directly injure the femoral nerve or compress it against the iliopsoas muscle or hip joint capsule. The described symptoms (quadriceps weakness, sensory loss in anterior thigh/medial leg, diminished patellar reflex) are classic signs of femoral nerve injury.
- **Embryology:** Derived from the lumbar plexus, which originates from the ventral rami of spinal nerves L1-L4.
- **Vascular Relationship:** The femoral artery passes deep to the inguinal ligament and runs medial to the femoral nerve within the femoral triangle. Injury to the femoral artery would present with signs of vascular compromise (absent femoral pulse, pallor, paresthesia, paralysis, poikilothermia) and potentially significant hemorrhage, which is not the primary presentation here, although it can co-occur.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Obturator nerve (L2-L4):** Passes through the obturator foramen. Innervates the adductor muscles of the thigh (adductor longus, brevis, magnus, gracilis, obturator externus) and provides sensory innervation to the medial thigh. Injury would cause weakness in thigh adduction and sensory loss over the medial thigh, not quadriceps weakness or anterior thigh/medial leg sensory loss.
- (B) **Sciatic nerve (L4-S3):** The largest nerve in the body, formed by the union of the tibial and common peroneal nerves. Exits the pelvis via the greater sciatic foramen, runs posterior to the hip joint, and descends down the posterior thigh. Innervates all muscles of the posterior thigh (hamstrings) and all muscles of the leg and foot. Injury would cause weakness in hamstring function (knee flexion, hip extension) and significant motor/sensory deficits in the entire leg and foot below the knee. This presentation is inconsistent with the patient's symptoms.
- (C) **Femoral artery:** The main arterial supply to the lower limb, passing deep to the inguinal ligament and through the femoral triangle. Injury would cause signs of vascular compromise (absent femoral pulse, pallor, paresthesia, paralysis, poikilothermia) and potentially massive hemorrhage. While it runs near the femoral nerve, the primary symptoms described (quadriceps weakness, specific sensory loss, reflex change) point specifically to nerve injury, not arterial injury.
- (E) **Superior gluteal nerve (L4-S1):** Exits the pelvis via the greater sciatic foramen, superior to the piriformis muscle. Innervates the gluteus medius, gluteus minimus, and tensor fasciae latae (hip abduction and internal rotation). Injury would cause weakness in hip abduction, leading to a Trendelenburg gait. This presentation is inconsistent with the patient's symptoms.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure             | Innervation/Function                                  | Sensory Distribution                               | Key Anatomical Relationship                                  | Clinical Presentation of Injury                                                                 |
|----------------------|-------------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| **Femoral Nerve (D)** | Quadriceps (knee ext.), Sartorius, Pectineus          | Anterior thigh, Medial leg (via saphenous nerve) | Passes under inguinal ligament, near iliopsoas, femoral artery | Quadriceps weakness, inability to extend knee, sensory loss anterior thigh/medial leg, ↓ patellar reflex |
| Obturator Nerve (A)   | Adductor muscles of thigh                             | Medial thigh                                        | Passes through obturator foramen                             | Medial thigh weakness/sensory loss                                                              |
| Sciatic Nerve (B)     | Hamstrings, all muscles below knee                    | Posterior thigh, entire leg/foot below knee         | Exits pelvis via greater sciatic foramen, posterior thigh      | Hamstring weakness, foot drop, sensory loss entire leg/foot                                   |
| Femoral Artery (C)    | Blood supply to lower limb                             | N/A                                                | Runs medial to femoral nerve in femoral triangle             | Absent femoral pulse, pallor, paresthesia, paralysis, poikilothermia, hemorrhage             |
| Sup. Gluteal Nerve (E) | Gluteus medius, minimus, TFL                          | Lateral hip                                         | Exits pelvis via greater sciatic foramen, superior piriformis | Weak hip abduction, Trendelenburg gait                                                         |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The femoral nerve's course through the psoas muscle and under the inguinal ligament makes it vulnerable to injury from pelvic fractures, retroperitoneal hematomas, or compression by structures like the iliopsoas muscle. Remember the classic presentation of femoral nerve injury: quadriceps weakness (inability to extend the knee), sensory loss over the anterior thigh and medial leg (saphenous nerve distribution), and a diminished patellar reflex. Differentiate this from injuries to the obturator nerve (medial thigh adduction/sensation), sciatic nerve (posterior thigh/leg/foot function/sensation), superior gluteal nerve (hip abduction), and femoral artery (vascular compromise).

<a id='question-003'></a>

### Question 003: Femoral Nerve Injury
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Femoral nerve (L2-L4) injury resulting from hip surgery, leading to quadriceps weakness (knee extension), iliopsoas/rectus femoris weakness (hip flexion), and loss of the patellar reflex (L4 dermatome/myotome). (股神经 (L2-L4) 损伤，因髋部手术引起，导致股四头肌无力 (膝关节伸展)，髂腰肌/股直肌无力 (髋关节屈曲)，以及髌腱反射消失 (L4 皮节/肌节))

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old male with a history of severe osteoarthritis undergoes a right total hip arthroplasty. Postoperatively, the patient complains of significant difficulty extending his right knee when walking uphill and rising from a seated position. Physical examination reveals 4/5 strength in the right quadriceps muscle (knee extension) and 3/5 strength in the right hip flexors (iliopsoas and rectus femoris). Sensory examination shows decreased sensation over the anterior thigh and medial leg. The patellar reflex (L4) is absent on the right side. Anteroposterior radiograph of the pelvis shows appropriate placement of the femoral head prosthesis. MRI of the right hip shows no evidence of hematoma or nerve compression. Which structure is most likely injured during the surgery, leading to these findings?

(A) Obturator nerve
(B) Sciatic nerve
(C) Superior gluteal nerve
(D) Femoral nerve
(E) Tibial nerve

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 68-year-old male, history of osteoarthritis, underwent right total hip arthroplasty. This sets the context for potential nerve injury during hip surgery.
- **Chief Complaint:** Difficulty extending the right knee (walking uphill, rising from seated). This points to weakness of the quadriceps femoris muscle group, the primary knee extensors.
- **Physical Exam - Motor:** 4/5 strength in right quadriceps (knee extension) and 3/5 strength in right hip flexors (iliopsoas and rectus femoris). This confirms weakness in muscles innervated by the femoral nerve. The rectus femoris is unique as it crosses both hip and knee joints and is innervated by both the femoral nerve (motor) and femoral nerve (sensory to the anterior thigh). Iliopsoas is innervated by the femoral nerve (L2-L3) and lumbar plexus branches (L1-L3).
- **Physical Exam - Sensory:** Decreased sensation over the anterior thigh and medial leg. This corresponds to the sensory distribution of the femoral nerve (anterior cutaneous branches of the femoral nerve supply the anterior thigh; the saphenous nerve, a branch of the femoral nerve, supplies the medial leg).
- **Physical Exam - Reflexes:** Absent patellar reflex (L4) on the right side. The patellar reflex tests the integrity of the L2-L4 spinal segments and the femoral nerve.
- **Imaging:** Radiograph shows proper prosthesis placement; MRI shows no obvious compression. This suggests direct injury during the surgical procedure rather than post-operative hematoma or other compression.
- **Conclusion:** The constellation of findings (quadriceps weakness, hip flexor weakness, anterior thigh/medial leg sensory loss, absent patellar reflex) following hip surgery strongly implicates injury to the femoral nerve.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Femoral Nerve (L2-L4):** The femoral nerve is the largest branch of the lumbar plexus. It originates from the posterior divisions of the L2, L3, and L4 spinal nerves. It passes under the inguinal ligament, lateral to the femoral artery, entering the anterior compartment of the thigh.
- **Motor Innervation:** It innervates the hip flexors (iliopsoas, pectineus, sartorius) and the knee extensors (quadriceps femoris: rectus femoris, vastus lateralis, vastus medialis, vastus intermedius). The rectus femoris also acts as a hip flexor.
- **Sensory Innervation:** It provides sensory innervation to the anterior thigh via its anterior cutaneous branches and to the medial leg via the saphenous nerve.
- **Reflex:** It mediates the patellar reflex (knee jerk reflex), testing the L2-L4 spinal segments.
- **Clinical Correlation:** Injury to the femoral nerve, often during hip surgery (especially anterior approaches) or femoral artery cannulation, results in the classic triad of quadriceps weakness (difficulty extending the knee), sensory loss over the anterior thigh and medial leg, and loss of the patellar reflex. Hip flexor weakness (iliopsoas, rectus femoris) is also characteristic. The patient's presentation perfectly matches this clinical picture.
- **Embryology:** The lumbar plexus, including the femoral nerve, develops from the ventral rami of spinal nerves L1-L4. The femoral nerve itself arises from the posterior divisions of L2, L3, and L4.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Obturator nerve (L2-L4):** This nerve passes through the obturator foramen to innervate the medial compartment of the thigh (adductor muscles: adductor longus, brevis, magnus, gracilis, obturator externus) and provides sensory innervation to the medial thigh. Injury would cause weakness in thigh adduction and sensory loss over the medial thigh, not quadriceps weakness or loss of the patellar reflex.
- (B) **Sciatic nerve (L4-S3):** This is the largest nerve in the body, originating from the sacral plexus (L4-S3). It runs down the posterior thigh, innervating the hamstrings (hip extension, knee flexion) and all muscles below the knee (via its tibial and common fibular branches). Injury would cause weakness in knee flexion, hip extension, ankle plantarflexion/dorsiflexion, and foot eversion/inversion, along with sensory loss over the posterior thigh, leg, and foot. This does not match the patient's presentation.
- (C) **Superior gluteal nerve (L4-S1):** This nerve arises from the sacral plexus (L4-S1) and innervates the gluteus medius, gluteus minimus, and tensor fasciae latae muscles, which are responsible for hip abduction and medial rotation. Injury would cause weakness in hip abduction (Trendelenburg gait) and sensory loss over the lateral hip. This does not match the patient's presentation.
- (E) **Tibial nerve (L4-S3):** This is a branch of the sciatic nerve, arising from the sacral plexus (L4-S3). It innervates the posterior compartment muscles of the leg (plantarflexors, invertors) and the intrinsic muscles of the foot. It provides sensory innervation to the posterior leg and the sole of the foot. Injury would cause weakness in plantarflexion and inversion, sensory loss in the posterior leg and sole, and loss of the Achilles reflex. This does not match the patient's presentation.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature             | Femoral Nerve (Correct) | Obturator Nerve | Sciatic Nerve | Superior Gluteal Nerve | Tibial Nerve |
|----------------------|--------------------------|-----------------|---------------|-------------------------|--------------|
| **Plexus Origin**    | Lumbar (L2-L4)           | Lumbar (L2-L4)  | Sacral (L4-S3) | Sacral (L4-S1)          | Sacral (L4-S3)|
| **Primary Muscles Innervated** | Quadriceps, Iliopsoas, Rectus Femoris, Sartorius, Pectineus | Adductors (Medial Thigh) | Hamstrings, Below Knee | Gluteus Medius, Minimus, TFL | Posterior Leg, Foot Intrinsic |
| **Key Motor Deficit** | Knee Extension Weakness | Thigh Adduction Weakness | Knee Flexion/Ankle/Foot Weakness | Hip Abduction Weakness | Ankle Plantarflexion Weakness |
| **Sensory Distribution** | Anterior Thigh, Medial Leg | Medial Thigh | Posterior Thigh, Leg, Foot | Lateral Hip | Posterior Leg, Sole |
| **Key Reflex**       | Patellar (L4)            | None specific  | Achilles (S1) | None specific           | Achilles (S1) |
| **Common Injury Site** | Hip Surgery, Femoral Artery Access | Pelvic Fractures, Hip Surgery | Piriformis Syndrome, Posterior Thigh Trauma | Hip Fracture, Intramuscular Injection | Ankle Trauma, Tarsal Tunnel Syndrome |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The femoral nerve (L2-L4) innervates the quadriceps femoris (knee extension), iliopsoas/rectus femoris (hip flexion), and provides sensation to the anterior thigh and medial leg. Injury, often during hip surgery, results in quadriceps weakness, hip flexor weakness, sensory loss in its distribution, and loss of the patellar reflex. Differentiating it from other lower limb nerve injuries requires understanding their specific motor, sensory, and reflex territories. Remember the mnemonic "Weak knee extension, weak hip flexion, sensory loss anterior thigh/medial leg, absent patellar reflex" for femoral nerve injury.

<a id='question-004'></a>

### Question 004: Femoral Nerve Injury & Sensory Deficit
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The femoral nerve (L2-L4) innervates the anterior thigh muscles (quadriceps femoris, sartorius, pectineus) and provides sensory innervation to the anteromedial thigh via the anterior femoral cutaneous nerve (AFCN) and the medial leg/foot via the saphenous nerve. Injury to the femoral nerve or its branches results in specific motor and sensory deficits. (股神经 (L2-L4) 支配前大腿肌肉并提供前内侧大腿的感觉支配 (前股外侧皮神经) 和内侧小腿/足的感觉支配 (大隐静脉神经)。股神经或其分支的损伤会导致特定的运动和感觉功能障碍。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of type 2 diabetes mellitus presents to the emergency department after a fall while gardening. He complains of severe pain in his right anterior thigh and inability to extend his right knee against gravity. Physical examination reveals significant tenderness over the right anterior hip region. Motor examination shows weakness (3/5 strength) in knee extension and hip flexion. Sensory examination reveals decreased sensation over the anterior and medial aspect of the right thigh, extending down to the medial aspect of the right lower leg and foot. Patellar reflex (L3-L4) is absent on the right side. Ankle reflexes (S1-S2) are intact bilaterally. An X-ray of the right hip shows no fracture. An MRI of the right hip and thigh reveals a hematoma compressing the femoral nerve as it passes under the inguinal ligament. Which of the following structures is MOST likely responsible for the sensory deficit observed on the medial aspect of the patient's right lower leg and foot?

(A) Obturator nerve
(B) Sciatic nerve
(C) Tibial nerve
(D) Saphenous nerve
(E) Sural nerve

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male, fall, right anterior thigh pain, inability to extend knee. Suggests injury to structures in the anterior thigh/hip region.
- **Past Medical History:** Type 2 diabetes mellitus. Increases risk for neuropathy, but the acute onset after trauma points towards a direct nerve injury.
- **Physical Examination:**
    - Tenderness over anterior hip: Localizes the injury site.
    - Weakness in knee extension (3/5): Indicates injury to the quadriceps femoris muscles, innervated by the femoral nerve.
    - Weakness in hip flexion (3/5): Indicates injury to the iliopsoas muscle (partially innervated by femoral nerve via the anterior femoral cutaneous nerve) and/or rectus femoris (innervated by femoral nerve).
    - Decreased sensation over anterior/medial thigh, medial lower leg, and foot: Points to involvement of sensory branches of the femoral nerve.
    - Absent patellar reflex (L3-L4): Confirms femoral nerve (L2-L4) involvement.
    - Intact ankle reflexes (S1-S2): Rules out significant sciatic nerve or tibial nerve involvement.
- **Imaging:** MRI shows hematoma compressing the femoral nerve under the inguinal ligament. Confirms femoral nerve injury.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the structure responsible for the sensory deficit on the medial lower leg and foot. This specific sensory distribution is key.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- The femoral nerve (L2, L3, L4) originates from the lumbar plexus in the psoas major muscle. It passes under the inguinal ligament, lateral to the femoral artery, into the thigh.
- In the thigh, it gives off several branches:
    - **Motor branches:** Innervate the anterior thigh muscles (quadriceps femoris, sartorius, pectineus).
    - **Sensory branches:**
        - **Anterior Femoral Cutaneous Nerve (AFCN):** Provides sensation to the anterolateral thigh (skin over the anterior thigh).
        - **Saphenous Nerve:** The largest cutaneous branch of the femoral nerve. It travels with the femoral artery and vein, passes through the adductor canal (Hunter's canal), and emerges medial to the knee. It provides sensation to the medial aspect of the lower leg and the medial aspect of the foot.
- The clinical presentation (weak knee extension, absent patellar reflex, sensory loss in anterior/medial thigh) confirms femoral nerve injury.
- The specific sensory deficit described (medial lower leg and foot) is characteristic of the saphenous nerve distribution.
- The saphenous nerve is a terminal sensory branch of the femoral nerve. Therefore, injury to the femoral nerve proximal to the origin of the saphenous nerve will result in this sensory deficit.
- **Embryology:** The femoral nerve arises from the ventral rami of spinal nerves L2-L4, consistent with its innervation pattern. The saphenous nerve, as a branch, follows this developmental origin.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Obturator nerve (L2-L4):** Passes through the obturator foramen to innervate the medial thigh muscles (adductors). It provides sensory innervation to a small area of the medial thigh, *not* the medial leg or foot. Injury causes weakness in thigh adduction and sensory loss in the medial thigh. Incorrect.
- (B) **Sciatic nerve (L4-S3):** The largest nerve in the body, formed by the tibial and common peroneal nerves. It runs down the posterior thigh, innervating the hamstrings and all muscles below the knee. It provides sensation to the posterior thigh, most of the leg, and the entire foot (except the medial aspect supplied by the saphenous nerve). Injury causes foot drop, weakness in plantarflexion/dorsiflexion, and widespread sensory loss in the leg and foot. Incorrect.
- (C) **Tibial nerve (L4-S3):** A branch of the sciatic nerve. It runs down the posterior leg, innervating the posterior compartment muscles (calf muscles) and providing sensation to the sole of the foot. Injury causes weakness in plantarflexion and sensory loss on the sole. Incorrect.
- (E) **Sural nerve (S1-S2):** Formed by branches of the tibial and common peroneal nerves. It runs down the posterolateral leg, providing sensation to the posterolateral aspect of the lower leg and the lateral aspect of the foot. Injury causes sensory loss in this distribution. Incorrect.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure          | Innervation (Motor)                                  | Sensory Distribution                                                                 | Clinical Correlation (Injury)                                                                                                                               |
| :----------------- | :--------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | :---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Femoral Nerve**  | Quadriceps, Sartorius, Pectineus, Iliopsoas (part) | Anterolateral thigh (AFCN), Medial leg & foot (Saphenous nerve)                       | Weak knee extension, weak hip flexion, absent patellar reflex, sensory loss in anterior thigh, medial leg, medial foot.                                       |
| Obturator Nerve    | Adductors (except hamstring part), Pectineus (part) | Medial thigh (small area)                                                              | Weak thigh adduction, sensory loss in medial thigh.                                                                                                        |
| Sciatic Nerve      | Hamstrings, all below knee                             | Posterior thigh, most of leg, lateral/plantar foot (via tibial/peroneal branches)     | Foot drop, weak plantarflexion/dorsiflexion, widespread sensory loss in leg/foot.                                                                        |
| Saphenous Nerve    | None (sensory only)                                 | Medial leg, medial foot                                                                | Sensory loss on medial leg and foot (often spared in femoral nerve injury if branch occurs distally, but included here as the *branch* causing the deficit). |
| Sural Nerve        | None (sensory only)                                 | Posterolateral leg, lateral foot                                                        | Sensory loss on posterolateral leg and lateral foot.                                                                                                       |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The femoral nerve (L2-L4) provides motor innervation to the anterior thigh muscles and sensory innervation via the anterior femoral cutaneous nerve (anterolateral thigh) and the saphenous nerve (medial leg and foot). Injury to the femoral nerve proximal to the origin of the saphenous nerve results in sensory loss in the medial leg and foot. Key differentials include the obturator nerve (medial thigh), sciatic nerve (posterior thigh/leg/foot), tibial nerve (sole of foot), and sural nerve (posterolateral leg/lateral foot).

<a id='question-005'></a>

### Question 005: Lower Limb Sensory Deficit after Varicose Vein Surgery
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The course of the saphenous nerve (a branch of the femoral nerve) and its relationship to the great saphenous vein (GSV) within the adductor canal (Hunter's canal), specifically its vulnerability to injury during surgical stripping of the GSV. (股神经的膝上股神经分支与大隐静脉在内收肌管内的关系，以及大隐静脉剥脱术中该神经受损的风险)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 55-year-old female presents to the clinic 6 weeks after undergoing bilateral lower extremity great saphenous vein stripping for symptomatic varicose veins. Post-operatively, she developed numbness and tingling along the medial aspect of her lower leg and foot. Physical examination reveals decreased sensation to light touch along the medial aspect of the lower leg, extending to the medial malleolus and the plantar surface of the foot, sparing the lateral aspect of the foot. Motor examination is normal, and reflexes are intact. Ankle reflexes (Achilles and plantar) are symmetric and normal. The patient denies any significant pain or weakness.

(A) Tibial nerve
(B) Sural nerve
(C) Saphenous nerve
(D) Common peroneal (fibular) nerve
(E) Superficial branch of the radial nerve

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 55-year-old female, post-operative (bilateral great saphenous vein stripping). This immediately points towards potential complications related to the surgery.
- **Symptoms:** Numbness and tingling (paresthesia) along the medial aspect of the lower leg and foot, developing post-operatively. This indicates a sensory nerve injury.
- **Physical Exam:** Decreased sensation to light touch along the medial lower leg, medial malleolus, and plantar foot. This specific sensory distribution is crucial. The sparing of the lateral foot is a key differentiator. Normal motor function and reflexes suggest a purely sensory nerve injury or a nerve with primarily sensory function in the affected area.
- **Procedure:** Great saphenous vein stripping. This procedure involves ligating and removing the GSV, often through incisions or tunnels along its course.
- **Deduction:** The medial lower leg and medial foot sensory distribution, coupled with the history of GSV stripping, strongly suggests injury to the nerve that runs alongside the GSV in that region.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Saphenous Nerve:** The saphenous nerve is the largest cutaneous branch of the femoral nerve (L2, L3, L4). It arises from the femoral nerve in the femoral triangle, typically just below the inguinal ligament.
- **Course:** It descends through the adductor canal (Hunter's canal), also known as the subsartorial canal, located deep to the sartorius muscle. Within the adductor canal, it runs *anterior* to the adductor longus tendon and *posterior* to the sartorius muscle. Crucially, it runs *alongside* the great saphenous vein (GSV) within this canal.
- **Innervation:** The saphenous nerve provides cutaneous innervation to the medial aspect of the lower leg and the medial side of the foot, including the plantar surface. This matches the patient's sensory deficit precisely.
- **GSV Stripping & Injury:** During GSV stripping, the vein is removed, often through incisions or tunnels. The saphenous nerve runs very close to the GSV within the adductor canal. Surgical manipulation, traction, or ligation during the procedure can easily injure or entrap the nerve, leading to the characteristic sensory deficit described.
- **Differential Diagnosis:** Other nerves in the lower leg have different distributions. The tibial nerve supplies the posterior compartment muscles and the plantar foot (lateral half and heel). The sural nerve supplies the posterolateral lower leg and lateral foot. The common peroneal (fibular) nerve supplies the lateral compartment muscles and the dorsum/lateral aspect of the foot. The superficial branch of the radial nerve supplies the dorsum of the hand and wrist, not the lower leg.
- **Embryology:** The femoral nerve, and thus the saphenous nerve, originates from the lumbar plexus (L2, L3, L4 ventral rami). The GSV develops from the posterior venous plexus of the leg. Their close anatomical relationship in the adductor canal is established during development.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Tibial nerve:** The tibial nerve (L4, L5, S1, S2, S3) is a terminal branch of the sciatic nerve. It runs down the posterior compartment of the leg, deep to the gastrocnemius and soleus muscles. It provides motor innervation to the posterior compartment muscles and sensory innervation to the posterior leg, the sole of the foot (lateral half), and the heel. Injury would cause sensory loss on the posterior leg and sole, and motor weakness in plantarflexion/inversion. This distribution does not match the patient's medial leg/foot sensory loss.
- (B) **Sural nerve:** The sural nerve (S1, S2) is formed by branches of the tibial and common peroneal nerves. It runs down the posterolateral aspect of the leg, superficial to the Achilles tendon. It provides sensory innervation to the posterolateral lower leg and the lateral aspect of the foot and ankle. Injury would cause sensory loss in this distribution, not the medial leg/foot.
- (D) **Common peroneal (fibular) nerve:** The common peroneal nerve (L4, L5, S1, S2) is the other terminal branch of the sciatic nerve. It wraps around the fibular head and divides into superficial and deep branches. It provides motor innervation to the anterior and lateral compartment muscles of the leg and sensory innervation to the anterolateral leg and the dorsum/lateral aspect of the foot. Injury (e.g., at the fibular head) causes foot drop and sensory loss on the dorsum/lateral foot. This does not match the patient's presentation.
- (E) **Superficial branch of the radial nerve:** This nerve (C6, C7, C8) is a branch of the radial nerve in the forearm. It provides sensory innervation to the dorsum of the hand and wrist. It has no relation to the lower leg or GSV stripping.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature             | Saphenous Nerve (Correct) | Tibial Nerve | Sural Nerve | Common Peroneal Nerve | Superficial Radial Nerve |
|----------------------|---------------------------|--------------|-------------|-----------------------|--------------------------|
| **Origin**           | Femoral Nerve (L2, L3, L4) | Sciatic (L4-S3) | Tib/Peroneal (S1, S2) | Sciatic (L4-S2)       | Radial (C6-C8)          |
| **Course**           | Femoral Triangle -> Adductor Canal (with GSV) | Posterior Leg | Posterolateral Leg   | Wraps Fibular Head     | Forearm                 |
| **Sensory Area**     | Medial Leg & Medial Foot | Posterior Leg & Plantar Foot (lateral) | Posterolateral Leg & Lateral Foot | Anterolateral Leg & Dorsum/Lateral Foot | Dorsum Hand & Wrist     |
| **Motor Function**   | None                      | Posterior Leg Muscles | None       | Anterior/Lateral Leg Muscles | None                    |
| **Clinical Relevance**| GSV Stripping Injury     | Sciatic Injury, Tarsal Tunnel Syndrome | Sciatic Injury, Heel Pad Injury | Fibular Head Fracture, Sciatic Injury | Wrist Drop, Hand Injury |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The saphenous nerve's close proximity to the great saphenous vein within the adductor canal makes it vulnerable to injury during vein stripping procedures. This results in sensory loss over the medial lower leg and medial foot. Remember to differentiate this distribution from the tibial (posterior leg/plantar foot), sural (posterolateral leg/lateral foot), and common peroneal (anterolateral leg/dorsum foot) nerves.

<a id='question-006'></a>

### Question 006: Obturator Nerve Injury
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The obturator nerve (L2-L4) exits the pelvis through the obturator canal, accompanied by the obturator artery and vein, and innervates the adductor muscles of the thigh and provides sensory innervation to the medial thigh. Injury to the nerve within the canal can occur due to pelvic fractures, surgery, or compression. (闭孔神经 (L2-L4) 穿行于闭孔管，伴有闭孔动脉和静脉，支配大腿内收肌群并提供大腿内侧的知觉。神经在管内的损伤可能由盆骨骨折、手术或压迫引起。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old female presents to the emergency department after a high-energy motor vehicle accident. She complains of severe left hip and thigh pain. On physical examination, she is unable to adduct her left leg against resistance. Sensory examination reveals decreased sensation over the medial aspect of her left thigh. Palpation of the left hip reveals tenderness over the anterior superior iliac spine (ASIS) and the region just inferior to the inguinal ligament. Anteroposterior (AP) radiographs of the pelvis show a comminuted fracture of the left acetabulum with displacement. Computed tomography (CT) scan of the pelvis confirms the acetabular fracture and reveals significant displacement of bone fragments near the obturator foramen. Which of the following structures is most likely injured in this patient?

(A) Femoral nerve
(B) Sciatic nerve
(C) Obturator nerve
(D) Superior gluteal nerve
(E) Pudendal nerve

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old female, high-energy MVA, left hip/thigh pain. This suggests trauma to the pelvic region.
- **Physical Exam:** Inability to adduct left leg against resistance (weakness of adductor muscles). Decreased sensation over the medial left thigh. These findings point towards a lesion affecting the motor supply to the adductors and sensory innervation of the medial thigh.
- **Imaging:** Comminuted left acetabular fracture with displacement near the obturator foramen. This anatomical location is crucial.
- **Deduction:** The combination of adductor weakness, medial thigh sensory loss, and a pelvic fracture near the obturator foramen strongly implicates the obturator nerve. The obturator nerve exits the pelvis through the obturator canal, which is located just inferior to the pubic crest and superior to the obturator membrane, adjacent to the acetabulum. Fractures in this area can directly injure the nerve or the vessels accompanying it.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Obturator Nerve (L2-L4):** Arises from the lumbar plexus (anterior divisions of L2, L3, L4 ventral rami).
- **Course:** Descends through the psoas major muscle, enters the pelvis, runs along the lateral wall of the pelvis, and exits the pelvis through the obturator canal (formed by the obturator membrane and the obturator internus muscle).
- **Companions:** Within the obturator canal, the obturator nerve is accompanied by the obturator artery and vein.
- **Innervation:**
    - **Motor:** Innervates the adductor muscles of the thigh (adductor longus, adductor brevis, adductor magnus [adductor part], gracilis, pectineus [sometimes also by femoral nerve]).
    - **Sensory:** Provides sensory innervation to the skin of the medial thigh (via the anterior and posterior cutaneous branches).
- **Clinical Correlation:** Injury to the obturator nerve, often due to pelvic fractures (especially acetabular fractures), hip dislocation, or surgery in the pelvic region, results in weakness of thigh adduction and sensory loss over the medial thigh. The patient's presentation perfectly matches this clinical picture. The fracture location near the obturator foramen directly threatens the nerve's exit path.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Femoral nerve:** Arises from the lumbar plexus (L2, L3, L4). Exits the abdomen via the psoas major muscle, passes under the inguinal ligament into the thigh. Innervates anterior thigh muscles (quadriceps femoris, sartorius, pectineus [sometimes]) and provides sensation to the anterior thigh and medial leg. Injury causes quadriceps weakness (difficulty extending the knee), hip flexion weakness (iliopsoas), and sensory loss in the anterior thigh/medial leg. This does not match the patient's adductor weakness and medial thigh sensory loss.
- (B) **Sciatic nerve:** Arises from the sacral plexus (L4-S3). Exits the pelvis through the greater sciatic foramen, runs down the posterior thigh. Innervates all muscles of the posterior thigh (hamstrings) and all muscles of the leg and foot (except tibialis anterior, extensor hallucis longus, extensor digitorum longus, which are innervated by the common peroneal/fibular nerve). Provides sensation to the posterior thigh, most of the leg, and the foot. Injury causes hamstring weakness, foot drop (if common peroneal division is affected), and sensory loss in the posterior leg/foot. This does not match the patient's presentation.
- (D) **Superior gluteal nerve:** Arises from the sacral plexus (L4-S1). Exits the pelvis through the greater sciatic foramen, superior to the piriformis muscle. Innervates the gluteus medius, gluteus minimus, and tensor fasciae latae muscles. Injury causes Trendelenburg gait (hip drop on the contralateral side during stance phase) due to gluteus medius weakness. It does not cause adductor weakness or medial thigh sensory loss.
- (E) **Pudendal nerve:** Arises from the sacral plexus (S2-S4). Exits the pelvis through the greater sciatic foramen, loops around the ischial spine, and enters the perineum through the lesser sciatic foramen. Provides motor innervation to perineal muscles (external anal sphincter, external urethral sphincter, bulbospongiosus, ischiocavernosus) and sensory innervation to the perineum, external genitalia, and anus. Injury causes loss of sensation in the perineum ("saddle anesthesia") and bowel/bladder dysfunction. This is unrelated to the patient's hip/thigh symptoms.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature             | Obturator Nerve (C) | Femoral Nerve (A) | Sciatic Nerve (B) | Superior Gluteal Nerve (D) | Pudendal Nerve (E) |
|----------------------|----------------------|--------------------|-------------------|-----------------------------|--------------------|
| **Plexus Origin**    | Lumbar (L2-L4)       | Lumbar (L2-L4)     | Sacral (L4-S3)    | Sacral (L4-S1)              | Sacral (S2-S4)    |
| **Exit Pelvis**      | Obturator Canal      | Psoas Major        | Greater Sciatic Foramen | Greater Sciatic Foramen (sup. piriformis) | Greater Sciatic Foramen |
| **Primary Motor**    | Thigh Adductors     | Knee Extensors, Hip Flexors | Hamstrings, Leg/Foot Muscles | Gluteus Medius/Minimus, TFL | Perineal Muscles |
| **Primary Sensory**  | Medial Thigh         | Anterior Thigh, Medial Leg | Posterior Thigh, Leg, Foot | Gluteal Region            | Perineum, Genitalia |
| **Clinical Deficit** | Adductor Weakness, Medial Thigh Sensory Loss | Quadriceps Weakness, Anterior Thigh Sensory Loss | Hamstring Weakness, Foot Drop, Leg/Foot Sensory Loss | Trendelenburg Gait | Saddle Anesthesia, Bowel/Bladder Dysfunction |
| **Vessel Companion** | Obturator Artery/Vein | -                  | -                 | -                           | -                  |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The obturator nerve (L2-L4) exits the pelvis via the obturator canal, accompanied by the obturator vessels, and innervates the medial thigh adductors and provides sensation to the medial thigh. Pelvic fractures, particularly acetabular fractures near the obturator foramen, are a common cause of injury. Key differentials include the femoral nerve (anterior thigh), sciatic nerve (posterior thigh/leg/foot), superior gluteal nerve (hip abductors), and pudendal nerve (perineum). Remember the specific motor and sensory distributions to differentiate these nerves.

---

<a id='question-007'></a>

### Question 007: Obturator Nerve Injury
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The obturator nerve (闭孔神经, bì kǒng shén jīng) is vulnerable to injury in the context of anterior pelvic fractures, pelvic lymphadenectomy, or obturator hernias due to its course through the obturator foramen (闭孔孔, bì kǒng kǒng) and its proximity to relevant anatomical structures. Injury typically results in adductor weakness and sensory loss over the medial thigh.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old male with a history of metastatic rectal cancer undergoes bilateral pelvic lymph node dissection. Postoperatively, he reports significant difficulty adducting his legs and complains of numbness along the medial aspect of both thighs. Physical examination reveals marked weakness (2/5 strength) in hip adduction bilaterally. Sensory testing shows decreased sensation to light touch over the medial thigh, extending from the groin down to the knee. Patellar and Achilles reflexes are normal (3+ bilaterally). Hip flexion and extension strength are normal (5/5). Ankle dorsiflexion and plantarflexion strength are also normal (5/5). An X-ray of the pelvis shows no acute fracture, but surgical clips are noted in the bilateral obturator regions.

Which nerve is most likely injured in this patient, leading to his clinical presentation?

(A) Femoral Nerve
(B) Sciatic Nerve
(C) Superior Gluteal Nerve
(D) Obturator Nerve
(E) Pudendal Nerve

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 68-year-old male with metastatic rectal cancer, post-bilateral pelvic lymph node dissection. This history points towards potential surgical complications involving pelvic structures.
- **Chief Complaint:** Difficulty adducting legs (weakness) and numbness on the medial thighs. This suggests a motor and sensory deficit affecting the adductor muscles and the medial thigh skin.
- **Physical Exam:**
    - Weakness (2/5) in hip adduction bilaterally. This directly implicates the muscles responsible for adduction (adductor longus, brevis, magnus, gracilis, pectineus).
    - Decreased sensation over the medial thigh. This points to a sensory nerve supplying this region.
    - Normal patellar reflex (L3-L4, femoral nerve). Rules out significant femoral nerve injury.
    - Normal Achilles reflex (S1-S2, tibial nerve component of sciatic nerve). Rules out significant sciatic nerve injury affecting the tibial component.
    - Normal hip flexion/extension (iliopsoas, gluteus maximus - femoral, obturator, sciatic nerves).
    - Normal ankle dorsiflexion/plantarflexion (tibialis anterior, gastrocnemius/soleus - deep peroneal, tibial nerves).
- **Imaging/Surgical Findings:** X-ray shows no fracture, but surgical clips are noted in the bilateral obturator regions, confirming the location of the surgery.
- **Deduction:** The combination of medial thigh sensory loss and profound hip adductor weakness, occurring after pelvic lymph node dissection in the obturator region, strongly points to injury of the nerve supplying the adductors and innervating the medial thigh.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Obturator Nerve (闭孔神经, bì kǒng shén jīng):**
    - **Origin:** Arises from the anterior divisions of the lumbar plexus (L2, L3, L4 ventral rami).
    - **Course:** Passes anteriorly through the pelvis, medial to the common iliac vessels, and exits the pelvis through the obturator foramen (闭孔孔, bì kǒng kǒng), passing between the obturator internus and externus muscles.
    - **Innervation:**
        - **Motor:** Supplies all the medial compartment muscles of the thigh: adductor longus, adductor brevis, adductor magnus (adductor part), gracilis, and obturator externus. Also supplies the pectineus muscle (which is sometimes considered part of the anterior compartment but functionally acts as an adductor).
        - **Sensory:** Provides sensory innervation to the skin of the medial thigh, extending from the groin down to the knee.
    - **Vulnerability:** Its course through the obturator foramen makes it susceptible to injury during pelvic surgeries (like lymphadenectomy), pelvic fractures (especially anterior column fractures), or obturator hernias (where abdominal contents protrude through the foramen, compressing the nerve).
    - **Clinical Presentation of Injury:** Weakness of hip adduction, sensory loss over the medial thigh. Hip flexion is usually spared (iliopsoas, innervated by femoral and obturator nerves, but iliopsoas is powerful), as is knee extension (quadriceps, femoral nerve).
    - **Embryology:** Derived from the lumbar plexus, which originates from the ventral rami of spinal nerves L1-L4.

- **Why other options are incorrect:**
    - **(A) Femoral Nerve (股神经, gǔ shén jīng):** Arises from L2, L3, L4. Supplies anterior thigh muscles (quadriceps femoris, sartorius, pectineus) and provides sensation to the anterior thigh and medial leg via the saphenous nerve. Injury causes quadriceps weakness (difficulty extending the knee) and sensory loss over the anterior thigh and medial leg. Patellar reflex would be diminished or absent. This does not match the patient's presentation.
    - **(B) Sciatic Nerve (坐骨神经, zuò gǔ shén jīng):** Largest nerve in the body, formed by the tibial and common peroneal nerves (L4-S3). Innervates posterior thigh muscles (hamstrings) and all muscles below the knee. Provides sensation to the posterior thigh, most of the leg, and the foot. Injury causes weakness in knee flexion and foot/ankle movements, and sensory loss in its distribution. Achilles reflex would be diminished or absent. This does not match the patient's presentation.
    - **(C) Superior Gluteal Nerve (上臀神经, shàng hùn shén jīng):** Arises from L4, L5, S1. Innervates gluteus medius, gluteus minimus, and tensor fasciae latae. Injury causes weakness in hip abduction and Trendelenburg gait. Sensation is primarily over the lateral hip. This does not match the patient's presentation.
    - **(E) Pudendal Nerve (会阴神经, huì yīn shén jīng):** Arises from S2-S4. Supplies the perineum, external genitalia, and external anal sphincter. Injury causes saddle anesthesia and bowel/bladder dysfunction. This does not match the patient's presentation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- **(A) Femoral Nerve:** Incorrect. Femoral nerve injury would cause weakness in knee extension (quadriceps) and sensory loss over the anterior thigh and medial leg. The patient has normal knee extension and sensory loss over the medial thigh, not the anterior thigh. The patellar reflex is also normal.
- **(B) Sciatic Nerve:** Incorrect. Sciatic nerve injury would cause weakness in knee flexion (hamstrings) and foot/ankle movements, along with sensory loss in the posterior thigh, leg, and foot. The patient has normal hip flexion/extension and ankle movements, and sensory loss is limited to the medial thigh. The Achilles reflex is normal.
- **(C) Superior Gluteal Nerve:** Incorrect. Superior gluteal nerve injury would cause weakness in hip abduction (gluteus medius/minimus) and sensory loss over the lateral hip. The patient has normal hip abduction strength and sensory loss over the medial thigh.
- **(E) Pudendal Nerve:** Incorrect. Pudendal nerve injury affects the perineum, causing saddle anesthesia and potential bowel/bladder dysfunction. The patient's symptoms are confined to the medial thigh.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature             | Obturator Nerve (D) | Femoral Nerve (A) | Sciatic Nerve (B) | Superior Gluteal Nerve (C) | Pudendal Nerve (E) |
|----------------------|----------------------|--------------------|-------------------|-----------------------------|--------------------|
| **Origin**           | L2, L3, L4           | L2, L3, L4         | L4-S3             | L4, L5, S1                  | S2-S4              |
| **Course**           | Obturator Foramen    | Under Inguinal Lig. | Pelvis -> Sciatic Notch | Through Piriformis -> Greater Sciatic Foramen | Pelvis -> Alcock's Canal |
| **Motor**            | Medial Thigh Adductors | Anterior Thigh Extensors | Posterior Thigh Flexors, Leg/Foot | Hip Abductors (Gluteus Medius/Minimus) | Perineal Muscles, External Genitalia |
| **Sensory**          | Medial Thigh          | Anterior Thigh, Medial Leg | Posterior Thigh, Leg, Foot | Lateral Hip                | Perineum, Saddle Area |
| **Key Muscle Weakness** | Hip Adduction         | Knee Extension      | Knee Flexion, Ankle/Foot | Hip Abduction               | Sphincter Control   |
| **Key Sensory Loss** | Medial Thigh          | Anterior Thigh      | Posterior Thigh/Leg | Lateral Hip                 | Saddle Area        |
| **Reflex**           | None specific         | Patellar (L3-L4)   | Achilles (S1-S2)  | None specific               | None specific      |
| **Clinical Context** | Pelvic Surgery/Fracture | Hip Fracture, Femoral Triangle | Hip Fracture, Sciatic Notch | Hip Fracture, Gait Issues | Perineal Trauma, Childbirth |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The obturator nerve (L2-L4) innervates the medial thigh adductor muscles and provides sensation to the medial thigh. It is vulnerable to injury during pelvic surgery (especially lymphadenectomy) or anterior pelvic fractures due to its passage through the obturator foramen. Injury results in weakness of hip adduction and sensory loss over the medial thigh. Differentiate this from femoral nerve injury (quadriceps weakness, anterior thigh sensory loss, absent patellar reflex), sciatic nerve injury (hamstring/lower leg weakness, posterior leg/foot sensory loss, absent Achilles reflex), superior gluteal nerve injury (hip abductor weakness, lateral hip sensory loss), and pudendal nerve injury (saddle anesthesia, perineal dysfunction).

<a id='question-008'></a>

### Question 008: Obturator Nerve Injury
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The obturator nerve innervates the adductor muscles of the thigh (adductor longus, brevis, magnus, gracilis, pectineus) and provides sensory innervation to the medial aspect of the thigh. Injury to this nerve results in weakness of hip adduction and sensory loss in its distribution. (闭孔神经支配大腿内收肌群（股长肌、股短肌、大收肌、缝匠肌、耻骨肌）并提供大腿内侧的感觉 innervation。该神经损伤会导致髋屈曲无力以及其分布区域的感觉丧失。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old female presents to the emergency department after sustaining a closed-book pelvic fracture during a fall down a flight of stairs. She complains of severe pain in her left hip and inability to adduct her left leg against resistance. Physical examination reveals significant tenderness over the left obturator foramen. Motor testing shows 4/5 strength in hip flexion (iliopsoas) and extension (gluteus maximus, hamstrings), but 0/5 strength in hip adduction (adductor longus, brevis, magnus). Sensory examination reveals decreased sensation over the medial aspect of the left mid-thigh. Patellar and Achilles reflexes are intact bilaterally. An X-ray confirms a displaced fracture of the left obturator foramen. Which of the following structures is most likely injured in this patient?

(A) Femoral nerve
(B) Sciatic nerve
(C) Obturator nerve
(D) Superior gluteal nerve
(E) Pudendal nerve

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old female, fall down stairs. This suggests a traumatic mechanism, potentially involving the pelvis.
- **Injury:** Closed-book pelvic fracture, specifically involving the obturator foramen. This is a critical anatomical location.
- **Symptoms:** Left hip pain, inability to adduct left leg against resistance. This points to motor weakness in the adductor muscle group.
- **Physical Exam:** Tenderness over the left obturator foramen. This localizes the injury site.
- **Motor Deficit:** 0/5 strength in hip adduction (adductor longus, brevis, magnus). This directly implicates the nerve supplying these muscles.
- **Sensory Deficit:** Decreased sensation over the medial aspect of the left mid-thigh. This corresponds to the sensory distribution of a specific nerve.
- **Reflexes:** Patellar (L2-L4, femoral nerve) and Achilles (S1-S2, tibial nerve) reflexes are intact, helping to rule out injuries to the femoral or sciatic nerves more proximally.
- **Imaging:** X-ray confirms fracture of the obturator foramen. This provides definitive anatomical localization of the injury.
- **Question:** Asks for the most likely injured structure.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Obturator Foramen:** A large opening in the hip bone, formed by the ischium and pubis. It transmits the obturator nerve, obturator artery, and obturator vein.
- **Obturator Nerve:** Arises from the lumbar plexus (L2, L3, L4 ventral rami). It exits the pelvis through the obturator foramen.
- **Motor Innervation:** Innervates the medial (adductor) compartment muscles of the thigh: adductor longus, adductor brevis, adductor magnus (adductor part), gracilis, and pectineus. These muscles are primarily responsible for hip adduction.
- **Sensory Innervation:** Provides sensory innervation to the skin on the medial aspect of the thigh, extending from the inguinal ligament down to about the knee joint line.
- **Clinical Correlation:** A fracture of the obturator foramen, as seen in this patient, directly puts the obturator nerve at high risk of injury. The clinical presentation of weakness in hip adduction (0/5 strength) and sensory loss over the medial thigh perfectly matches the function of the obturator nerve.
- **Embryology:** The obturator nerve originates from the lumbar plexus, which develops from the ventral rami of spinal nerves L1-L4.
- **Kaplan/First Aid Reference:** Kaplan Preclinical Anatomy Review 2023, pp. 202–213 details the lumbar plexus, obturator nerve course, innervation, and clinical correlations. First Aid 2025 (Musculoskeletal: Lower Limb Anatomy & Nerve Lesions) also covers the obturator nerve and its deficits.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Femoral nerve:** Arises from the lumbar plexus (L2, L3, L4). It passes under the inguinal ligament into the anterior thigh. It innervates the anterior thigh muscles (quadriceps femoris, sartorius, pectineus) and provides sensory innervation to the anterior thigh and medial leg (via the saphenous nerve). Injury would cause weakness in knee extension and hip flexion (iliopsoas contribution), and sensory loss over the anterior thigh and medial leg. The patellar reflex would be lost. This does not match the patient's presentation (adductor weakness, medial thigh sensory loss, intact patellar reflex).
- (B) **Sciatic nerve:** Arises from the sacral plexus (L4-S3). It exits the pelvis through the greater sciatic foramen, passes through the posterior thigh, and divides into the tibial and common peroneal nerves. It innervates the posterior thigh muscles (hamstrings) and all muscles below the knee. Injury would cause weakness in knee flexion and ankle/foot movements, and sensory loss over the posterior thigh, lateral leg, and foot. This does not match the patient's presentation.
- (D) **Superior gluteal nerve:** Arises from the sacral plexus (L4, L5, S1). It exits the pelvis through the greater sciatic foramen and innervates the gluteus medius, gluteus minimus, and tensor fasciae latae muscles. Injury would cause weakness in hip abduction and internal rotation, leading to a Trendelenburg gait. Sensory innervation is minimal (skin over the upper lateral thigh). This does not match the patient's presentation.
- (E) **Pudendal nerve:** Arises from the sacral plexus (S2, S3, S4). It exits the pelvis through the greater sciatic foramen, loops around the ischial spine, and re-enters the pelvis through the lesser sciatic foramen. It innervates the perineal muscles (external anal sphincter, external urethral sphincter, bulbospongiosus, ischiocavernosus) and provides sensory innervation to the perineum and external genitalia. Injury would cause saddle anesthesia and bowel/bladder dysfunction. This does not match the patient's presentation.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature             | Obturator Nerve (Correct) | Femoral Nerve (A) | Sciatic Nerve (B) | Superior Gluteal Nerve (D) | Pudendal Nerve (E) |
|----------------------|---------------------------|--------------------|-------------------|-----------------------------|--------------------|
| **Origin**           | Lumbar Plexus (L2-L4)     | Lumbar Plexus (L2-L4)| Sacral Plexus (L4-S3)| Sacral Plexus (L4-S1)      | Sacral Plexus (S2-S4)|
| **Exit Pelvis**      | Obturator Foramen         | Inguinal Ligament  | Greater Sciatic Foramen| Greater Sciatic Foramen    | Greater Sciatic Foramen -> Lesser Sciatic Foramen|
| **Motor Innervation**| Medial Thigh (Adductors)  | Anterior Thigh (Quads, Sartorius)| Posterior Thigh (Hamstrings), Leg/Foot| Gluteus Medius/Minimus, TFL| Perineum, External Sphincters|
| **Sensory Innervation**| Medial Mid-Thigh          | Anterior Thigh, Medial Leg| Posterior Thigh, Lateral Leg, Foot| Upper Lateral Thigh (minimal)| Perineum, External Genitalia|
| **Key Deficit**      | Hip Adduction Weakness, Medial Thigh Sensory Loss| Knee Extension Weakness, Patellar Reflex Loss| Knee Flexion/Ankle/Foot Weakness| Hip Abduction Weakness, Trendelenburg Gait| Saddle Anesthesia, Bowel/Bladder Dysfunction|
| **Clinical Scenario** | Pelvic/Obturator Foramen Fracture| Anterior Thigh Injury, Hip Flexion Weakness| Posterior Thigh Injury, Sciatica| Hip Abductor Weakness| Perineal Injury|

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The obturator nerve exits the pelvis via the obturator foramen and innervates the medial compartment muscles of the thigh (adductors) and provides sensation to the medial thigh. Injury to this nerve, often due to pelvic fractures involving the obturator foramen, results in weakness of hip adduction and sensory loss over the medial thigh. Differentiating it from femoral nerve (anterior thigh, knee extension), sciatic nerve (posterior thigh, leg/foot), superior gluteal nerve (hip abduction), and pudendal nerve (perineum) relies on understanding their specific anatomical courses, innervation patterns, and clinical presentations.

<a id='question-009'></a>

### Question 009: Lateral Femoral Cutaneous Nerve Injury
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The lateral femoral cutaneous nerve (LFCN), a pure sensory nerve derived from the lumbar plexus (L2, L3), supplies sensation to the anterolateral thigh. Its course under the inguinal ligament near the anterior superior iliac spine (ASIS) makes it vulnerable to injury during procedures or trauma in this region. (股外侧皮神经 (L2, L3), 纯感觉神经, 来源于腰丛, 为股外侧供应感觉。其在耻骨前上肠骨棘 (ASIS) 附近 inguinal ligament 下方的走行使其容易在该区域的手术或外伤中受伤。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old female presents to the emergency department after sustaining a fall while hiking. She reports immediate pain in her right anterior thigh and difficulty walking. Physical examination reveals tenderness to palpation over the right anterior superior iliac spine (ASIS). Sensory testing demonstrates numbness and paresthesia in the anterolateral aspect of the right thigh, extending from the hip to the knee. Motor examination of the hip flexors (iliopsoas, rectus femoris, sartorius), knee extensors (quadriceps femoris), and ankle dorsiflexors (tibialis anterior) is normal (strength 5/5). Patellar and Achilles reflexes are intact bilaterally. An X-ray of the pelvis and femur is negative for fracture. Which of the following structures is most likely injured?

(A) Femoral nerve
(B) Obturator nerve
(C) Sciatic nerve
(D) Lateral femoral cutaneous nerve
(E) Superior gluteal nerve

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 32-year-old female, fall, right anterior thigh pain, difficulty walking. This suggests a potential injury to a nerve supplying the anterior thigh or affecting hip/knee function.
- **Physical Examination:** Tenderness over the right ASIS. This is a key landmark near the course of the LFCN.
- **Sensory Deficit:** Numbness and paresthesia in the anterolateral thigh (hip to knee). This specific sensory distribution is characteristic of the LFCN.
- **Motor Examination:** Normal strength in hip flexors, knee extensors, and ankle dorsiflexors. This rules out significant injury to the femoral nerve (hip flexion, knee extension), sciatic nerve (knee flexion, ankle dorsiflexion/plantarflexion), and superior gluteal nerve (hip abduction).
- **Reflexes:** Intact patellar (L2-L4, femoral nerve) and Achilles (S1-S2, tibial nerve) reflexes. This further supports the exclusion of major femoral or sciatic nerve involvement.
- **Imaging:** Negative X-ray rules out fracture as the primary cause of symptoms.
- **Conclusion:** The combination of localized tenderness over the ASIS, a pure sensory deficit in the anterolateral thigh, and preserved motor function strongly points to an injury of the LFCN.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Lateral Femoral Cutaneous Nerve (LFCN):**
    - **Origin:** Lumbar plexus, specifically from the ventral rami of L2 and L3 spinal nerves. (腰丛, L2和L3脊神经前束)
    - **Course:** Arises from the lumbar plexus, passes posterior to the psoas major muscle, enters the thigh, and runs laterally. It then passes *under* the inguinal ligament (Poupart's ligament) near the ASIS. (从腰丛发出，穿过大腰肌后方，进入大腿，然后向外侧走行。然后它*穿过*耻骨韧带 (Poupart's ligament) 靠近 ASIS。)
    - **Innervation:** Purely sensory. Supplies the skin of the anterolateral thigh, extending from the hip joint to the knee joint. (纯感觉神经。为股外侧大腿的皮肤供感，从髋关节延伸到膝关节。)
    - **Vulnerability:** Its superficial course under the inguinal ligament near the ASIS makes it susceptible to injury from trauma (falls, direct blows), surgical procedures (e.g., inguinal hernia repair, hip arthroplasty), or compression (e.g., tight clothing, obesity). (其在 ASIS 附近 inguinal ligament 下方的浅表走行使其容易受到外伤 (跌倒、直接撞击)、手术 (例如 inguinal 疝修补术、髋关节置换术) 或压迫 (例如紧身衣物、肥胖) 的伤害。)
    - **Clinical Presentation (Meralgia Paresthetica):** Injury or compression of the LFCN typically causes burning pain, numbness, tingling, or altered sensation in the anterolateral thigh. Motor function is unaffected because the LFCN is purely sensory. (LFCN 的损伤或压迫通常会导致股外侧大腿的灼烧痛、麻木、刺痛或感觉改变。运动功能不受影响，因为 LFCN 是纯感觉神经。)
- **Comparison with other options:**
    - **Femoral Nerve (A):** Supplies motor function to quadriceps femoris (knee extension) and iliopsoas (hip flexion), and sensory function to the anterior thigh and medial leg. Injury would cause quadriceps weakness and sensory loss in a different distribution. Patellar reflex would be diminished or absent.
    - **Obturator Nerve (B):** Supplies motor function to adductor muscles of the thigh and sensory function to the medial thigh. Injury would cause adductor weakness and sensory loss on the medial thigh.
    - **Sciatic Nerve (C):** Supplies motor function to hamstrings (knee flexion) and all muscles below the knee, and sensory function to the posterior thigh, leg, and foot. Injury would cause significant motor and sensory deficits in the lower leg and foot.
    - **Superior Gluteal Nerve (E):** Supplies motor function to gluteus medius, gluteus minimus, and tensor fasciae latae (hip abduction). Injury would cause weakness in hip abduction and Trendelenburg gait.

##### 3. Embryological Origin (胚胎发生)
- The LFCN, like all spinal nerves, originates from the spinal cord and travels through the intervertebral foramina. It joins the lumbar plexus, which forms from the ventral rami of L1-L4 spinal nerves. The LFCN specifically arises from L2 and L3. The sensory fibers within the LFCN originate from the dorsal root ganglia of L2 and L3.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- **(A) Femoral nerve:** Incorrect. The femoral nerve (L2, L3, L4) provides motor innervation to the quadriceps femoris (knee extension) and iliopsoas (hip flexion). Sensory innervation is to the anterior thigh and medial leg. The patient has intact motor function in the quadriceps and hip flexors, and the sensory loss is anterolateral, not anterior/medial. The patellar reflex (mediated by the femoral nerve) is also intact.
- **(B) Obturator nerve:** Incorrect. The obturator nerve (L2, L3, L4) innervates the adductor muscles of the thigh and provides sensation to the medial thigh. The patient has no motor weakness in adduction and the sensory loss is anterolateral, not medial.
- **(C) Sciatic nerve:** Incorrect. The sciatic nerve (L4, L5, S1, S2, S3) is the largest nerve in the body, innervating the posterior thigh, leg, and foot. Injury would cause significant motor and sensory deficits below the knee, which are absent in this patient.
- **(E) Superior gluteal nerve:** Incorrect. The superior gluteal nerve (L4, L5, S1) innervates the gluteus medius, minimus, and tensor fasciae latae, responsible for hip abduction. Injury would cause weakness in hip abduction and a Trendelenburg gait. The patient's symptoms are localized to the anterolateral thigh sensation, with no motor deficits related to hip abduction.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature                 | Lateral Femoral Cutaneous Nerve (LFCN) | Femoral Nerve             | Obturator Nerve          | Sciatic Nerve            | Superior Gluteal Nerve |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------- | :------------------------ | :----------------------- | :----------------------- | :--------------------- |
| **Origin**              | Lumbar Plexus (L2, L3)                 | Lumbar Plexus (L2, L3, L4) | Lumbar Plexus (L2, L3, L4) | Sacral Plexus (L4-S3)    | Sacral Plexus (L4-S1)  |
| **Function**            | Sensory only                           | Motor & Sensory           | Motor & Sensory          | Motor & Sensory          | Motor only             |
| **Motor Innervation**   | None                                   | Quadriceps, Iliopsoas     | Adductors               | Hamstrings, Below Knee   | Gluteus Medius/Minimus |
| **Sensory Innervation** | Anterolateral Thigh                    | Anterior Thigh, Medial Leg | Medial Thigh            | Posterior Thigh, Leg/Foot | Gluteal Region         |
| **Key Course**          | Under Inguinal Ligament near ASIS       | Passes under inguinal ligament, enters thigh | Passes under inguinal ligament, enters thigh | Exits pelvis via greater sciatic foramen | Exits pelvis via greater sciatic foramen |
| **Clinical Injury**     | Meralgia Paresthetica                   | Quadriceps Weakness, Sensory Loss | Adductor Weakness, Sensory Loss | Foot Drop, Sensory Loss | Trendelenburg Gait    |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The lateral femoral cutaneous nerve (LFCN), a pure sensory nerve from L2/L3, supplies the anterolateral thigh. Its superficial course under the inguinal ligament near the ASIS makes it vulnerable to injury, causing meralgia paresthetica (sensory symptoms in the anterolateral thigh) without motor deficits. Differentiating LFCN injury from femoral, obturator, sciatic, or superior gluteal nerve injuries relies on recognizing the specific sensory distribution and the absence of motor weakness corresponding to those nerves' functions.
